# Artifact inventory

Every artifact copied into artifacts/ of this repository and of OpenSpineConsortium/symphony-template, read as a file on 2026-10-22 by the thread "Artifact Textbook" (issue #120): what each one is, when it is from, the question it answers, its claims with a grade hint, its numbers with their units, the datasets, tools and models it names, its decisions, its open questions and the artifacts it relates to. Each record was written by a reader that saw only the artifact's own text, as data and never as instructions, and quoted it as evidence; nothing was filled from memory. No e-mail address, phone number or home address is reproduced, and people are named by role.

70 artifacts inventoried (54 in SchwingNet, 16 in symphony-template); 3 skipped as not copied. Generated by the synthesis of 2026-10-22 from inventory.json, with 2 records read since by .github/workflows/synthesis.yml, the latest on 2026-10-29 (the records they replace are in work/out/inventory-history.json); edit the JSON and render again rather than editing this file.

## Records

One block per artifact: the question, the summary, the key claims with their grade hint, the numbers that carry the strongest claims, the decisions, the open questions and the related artifacts. The full lists are in inventory.json.

### 1. AIDaS Seed Grant Proposal

- Folder: `SchwingNet/artifacts/aidas-seed-grant-proposal`; account: first account; link id: UwoqsEAjFPcUJVDutVpFHx; page, grant; date: 2026-10 (No authoring date is stated. The header names the "2026-2027 Cycle" and the submission is due "by October 16, 2026, 1...); confidentiality: mixed.
- Question: What seed project would validate exact intraoperative vertebral counting on the hospital's own C-arm in 2027, and what preliminary results, budget and federal pathway support it?
- Summary: A seed grant proposal for the AIDaS 2026-2027 cycle requesting $20,000 for calendar 2027 to validate exact intraoperative vertebral counting on the C-arm the hospital already owns. It motivates the work with registry, survey, litigation and cost figures on wrong-level spine surgery and argues that the safeguards in use are themselves counts. The method counts rather than names: an anchored first exposure at the sacrum or C2, the C-arm stepped one field minus two bodies, overlapping frames linked by normalised cross-correlation within 0.6 of a body of the commanded step, and a vertebral-body and anchor detector as the only learned component, with a physics renderer of fluoroscopy from CT for training. Preliminary results report exact counts on 99.17-100 % of reached walks and identify detection, not counting, as the deployment risk (99.14 % per-body recall on real films vs 71.67 % render-only), with the renderer explaining 80.9 % of real-frame variance on DeepFluoro. The 2027 plan runs an IRB-approved retrospective fluoroscopy corpus, phantom sweeps, cadaver validation, a Jetson-based frame-grabber prototype, a shadow-mode operating-room study and an FDA pre-submission, leading to an NIH NIBIB R21; the faculty PI slot, several cover-page facts and budget lines 1 to 5 are flagged to confirm.
- Key claims:
  - (inferred) Wrong-level spine surgery persists because a C-arm shows a few vertebrae at a time and the surgeon counts from whatever landmark is visible.
  - (reported) In a registry of 34,544 degenerative spine operations the wrong-level incidence was 0.12 %, with intraoperative disorientation the cause in 81.3 %.
  - (reported) The safeguards in use are themselves counts; site-verification protocols failed to prevent about one-third of cases.
  - (inferred) Avoiding the count with preoperative fiducials costs about an hour and 183 mGy each, about $1,750-1,950 of operating-room time per placement.
  - (unverified) No verified capital cost for a navigation suite, no study showing navigation reduces wrong-level surgery, and no measure of surgeon workload with versus without navigation are known.
  - (inferred) The innovation is to count, not name: the only learned component is a detector, nothing learned decides the level, and there is no tracker, reference frame, preoperative CT or capital equipment.
  - (measured) On public labelled CT renders and real radiographs the count was exact on 99.17-100 % of reached walks in twelve arms.
  - (measured) The deployment risk is detection, not counting: a render-only detector reaches 71.67 % per-body recall against 99.14 % for one trained on real films, and exactness falls from 100 % to 30.06 % with its boxes.
  - (measured) On DeepFluoro the renderer explains 80.9 % of a real frame's variance, nine calibrated device terms add 4.3 points, and a per-frame smooth field reaches 93.9 % against a 99.5 % quantum-noise ceiling.
  - (measured) A detector trained only on calibrated renders of DeepFluoro's own CTs finds the sacrum on about 96-98 % of real frames; trained on renders of living whole-body CTs it reaches about 64-77 %.
  - (inferred) Synthetic fluoroscopy from CT is close in physics but tuned to one C-arm and one cadaveric population, so in-domain real fluoroscopy is required.
  - (inferred) The existing Not Human Participant Research determination covers public de-identified data only, so the retrospective fluoroscopy review needs a new IRB protocol.
- Numbers (51 recorded; the first 10 here):

  - October 16, 2026, 11:59 p.m. ET deadline: submission of the single PDF per the RFP

### 2. Retrospective Fluoroscopy Protocol

- Folder: `SchwingNet/artifacts/retrospective-fluoroscopy-protocol`; account: first account; link id: 8sz8XUJsAdDDWZdKBCt5sc; page, protocol; date: 2027-01 (The form itself says "Date Submitted: Draft" and carries no date of its own. The latest dates it mentions are the pla...); confidentiality: mixed.
- Question: What does the draft WSU IRB eProtocol say, field by field, for retrospectively collecting archived DMC intraoperative fluoroscopy to fine-tune and test the vertebral-body detector of the level-counting method, and why is Expedited Category 5 requested?
- Summary: A draft WSU IRB eProtocol, mirroring the print view of a precedent protocol (IRB-21-10-4123), for the retrospective collection of archived intraoperative C-arm still images and cine loops from spine operations by one DMC neurosurgeon between 2021 and 2026, to fine-tune and test the vertebral-body detector at the front of the owner's level-counting method in the domain where it would be used. It fills every form section: the checklist (FDA-support box checked, waiver of consent, DMC ancillary review, PHI with a waiver of authorization), funding (none; an AIDaS seed grant application planned), the lay summary, three aims and three hypotheses, a background of cited wrong-level surgery rates and costs, the method under study, inclusion and exclusion, a patient-level split with a sealed test set, a primary endpoint of per-body detector recall (about 1,100 labelled bodies for 97% recall at a 1-point half-width) with secondary count-exactness, decline, agreement and failure endpoints, procedures in order, confidentiality, HIPAA identifiers and attachments. An attached protocol document argues for Expedited Category 5 over Exempt Category 4 and outlines a separate planned follow-on prospective shadow-mode study (Protocol 2) that is not part of this submission. Many fields are marked [to confirm], pending a pilot PACS query, the PI decision and institutional requirements.
- Key claims:
  - (reported) Wrong-level spine surgery occurred in 0.12% of 34,544 registry operations, with intraoperative disorientation the cause in 81.3%.
  - (measured) The counting method was exact on 99.17-100% of reached walks across twelve arms on public CT renders and real radiographs.
  - (inferred) The only learned component of the method is the vertebral-body and anchor detector; nothing learned decides the level and no name is formed.
  - (measured) The deployment risk is the detector: film-trained recall is 99.14% but render-trained is 71.67%, and render-trained boxes dropped count exactness from 100% to 30.06%.
  - (measured) Synthetic fluoroscopy from CT does not close the domain gap: a physics renderer explains 80.9% of a real DeepFluoro frame's variance and render-trained sacrum detection drops to 64-77% on other CTs.
  - (inferred) Performance on this institution's own C-arms and patients must be measured before any prospective study starts.
  - (inferred) H1: a detector fine-tuned on DMC fluoroscopy will reach at least 97% per-body recall on held-out DMC cases.
  - (inferred) About 1,100 labelled bodies in the held-out set are needed to estimate 97% recall with a 1-point half-width.
  - (inferred) Expedited Category 5 is requested rather than Exempt Category 4 because research that may support an FDA submission is not eligible for exemption and the team handles identifiable images and a coded master list.
  - (reported) The earlier WSU IRB Not Human Participant Research determination covers public de-identified datasets only, not PACS data.
  - (inferred) Pre-operative fiducial placement costs roughly $1,750-1,950 per placement in 2014 dollars, from a mean 51.9 minutes at Mayo and FY2014 operating-room cost per minute.
  - (unverified) The literature holds no verified capital cost of a navigation suite, no study that navigation reduces wrong-level surgery, and no measure of surgeon workload with versus without navigation.

### 3. Fluoroscopy Data Atlas

- Folder: `SchwingNet/artifacts/fluoroscopy-data-atlas`; account: first account; link id: 8YNXyLYbthsLjqEbFxQph2; page, atlas; date: 2026-10-17 (Header chip "C-arm level identification . data . as of 17 October 2026"; the sources line says the web search was mad...); confidentiality: schwingnet-other.
- Question: Which fluoroscopy, spine radiograph, paired CT-X-ray, CBCT projection and DRR datasets exist for intraoperative vertebral level identification, which are already on the grid, which need a request letter, and which ten gaps are worth getting next?
- Summary: An interactive catalog, as of 17 October 2026, of 217 datasets (189 from the project's own records, 28 found only on the web) sorted into seven kinds (fluoroscopy video, C-arm frames, paired CT and real X-ray, CBCT projections, paired CT and synthetic DRR, radiographs, by request only), each with anatomy, size, annotation level, level labels, pose, access, licence, grid path and notes, and a status: held on the grid (52), request ready (16) or not held (149). A 'Worth getting next' list ranks ten gaps by value to CT-to-C-arm parity (COVID-19-NY-SBU, LUMOS, MIDRC, Balgrist, UCLA kV/MV, PENGWIN, SyntheX, SPARE, LIDC-IDRI, a femur set). Sixteen request letters are ready, eleven also as unsent Outlook drafts; Radiopaedia's seven multi-frame cases are the most appealing but carry a reported USD 250 fee, non-commercial terms and a ban on scripted downloading. A checklist before sending covers commercial use, IRB, training promises, co-authorship and YouTube copies. The entries also record defects in the project's own banks (duplicate persons across splits, wrong row spacing, unused real frames).
- Key claims:
  - (measured) 217 datasets were found, 189 from the project's records and 28 only on the web, with 16 request letters.
  - (measured) Of the 217, 52 are held on the grid, 16 have a letter drafted and 149 are not held (counted from the page's embedded data).
  - (measured) Sixteen sources can only be had by asking; eleven letters also exist as unsent Outlook drafts.
  - (unverified) Radiopaedia's application carries a non-refundable fee recorded as USD 250 that could not be confirmed from public sources.
  - (reported) Radiopaedia's licence is non-commercial and bars scripted downloading, so validating the 510(k) product would need a separately negotiated licence.
  - (inferred) The Balgrist ex-vivo set (13 torsos, about 1,000 real shots on three C-arms with fiducial poses) is the best fit that exists.
  - (measured) C-ArmLSpine's 602 raw sweep projections are used by no model, and case39's sweep is in no root.
  - (measured) COBRA2026 holds 692 public training cases of 867, 375,618 projections, 771.1 GB, with no anatomical labels.
  - (measured) The anchor_drr bank's own split puts 301 of 801 CTSP people on both sides.
  - (inferred) SX-Stitch is the only published collection of overlapping intraoperative C-arm spine frames, the acquisition the sweep protocol simulates.
  - (inferred) Lateral lumbar flexion-extension at video frame rate is found nowhere public.
  - (measured) FluoroCap is mostly reproductions of published figures and its 21,603 manifest rows all have human QC pending.

### 4. SchwingNet grid pipeline

- Folder: `SchwingNet/artifacts/schwingnet-grid-pipeline`; account: first account; link id: UMnwCkV9SQm2kokkCXWUKA; page, board; date: 2026-10-21 (Header 'SchwingNet . private . 2026-10-21'; the schedule marks 'Wed 21 Oct Today'; spine-datasets 7a74bb6 dated 2026-...); confidentiality: mixed.
- Question: How does a SchwingNet run start on the Wayne State grid without the owner typing a command, what does each job wait for, and what still stands in the way?
- Summary: A private status page for SchwingNet's grid pipeline on 21 October 2026. The gate words are written and under review in SchwingNet PR #54 and no job has run yet; every number so far comes from synthetic CPU runs. A run starts when Claude, from a cloud session, sends one gate word through symphony-template's grid-accounts.yml to the Beethoven gate on the login node, which runs grid/gate.sh, submits Slurm jobs and returns at most 200 kB on the branch grid; one press by the owner remains, Connect GitHub Actions in Beethoven once beethoven-extension #23 reaches him. The start word submits the whole dependency graph (weights, C-ArmLSpine ingest, first catalog, version 1 on GPU, setup when pixi.lock changed, the multi-day X-ray and CT fetch chains, a disk probe), and seven words are everything Claude can ask. Counts are tabled: 111 registry sources by reach, 86 spine-datasets records by status, 22 training registry entries without a shard, 5 of 20 bank recipes ready, fetch list sizes including CT-RATE's 21.3 TB switched off. A schedule runs to the provisional on 30 October and the CNS talk on 2 November, and a ledger lists what waits on the owner, Claude, PhysioNet, Radiopaedia and JHU.
- Key claims:
  - (measured) The gate words are written and under review in PR #54; no job has run on the grid yet.
  - (measured) Every grid number on the page is a wall-time limit or a stated size, not a measurement; all measured numbers so far are from synthetic CPU runs.
  - (inferred) The owner types nothing on the grid: Claude sends one gate word and reads the answer as a file of at most 200 kB on the branch grid.
  - (measured) One press by the owner remains, Connect GitHub Actions in Beethoven after beethoven-extension #23; no word can run before it.
  - (inferred) One start word submits the whole graph with Slurm dependencies, and the version 1 path no longer waits on the multi-day X-ray fetch.
  - (measured) Each fetch chain refuses to submit when the sizes it states exceed the volume's free space minus 5 TB.
  - (measured) Seven gate words are everything Claude can ask of the grid, each refusing a bad argument, a second word while one runs, and a failed squeue.

- Folder: `SchwingNet/artifacts/schwingnet-data-registry`; account: first account; link id: L4pJez6yLFzaX8x5jiA3JL; page, registry; date: 2026-10-20 (Header line: 'SchwingNet, data/sources, 60 records, rendered 2026-10-20'.); confidentiality: schwingnet-other.
- Question: What does each of the 60 dataset sources recorded under SchwingNet's data/sources hold, what vertebral level truth does it carry, and how far does the project reach it (held, to get, gated, embargoed, nonexistent or excluded)?
- Summary: A rendered registry of the 60 source records under SchwingNet's data/sources, one row per dataset, grouped as real intraoperative fluoroscopy stills, video and cine, projections with a registered CT, plain films, CT collections and renders, and future directions. Each row gives what the source is, its level truth, a reach word (HAVE, GET, GATED, EMBARGOED, NONE, EXCLUDED), a status, the records' counts, and on opening the licence, the link, the grid path, the steps to get what is not held and the spine-program record it was taken from. The page says its numbers are the records' numbers, that the Markdown page under docs/research is rendered from the same files, and that sample-level counts stay empty until a grid job runs on the owner's word. By my tally of the records, 24 are held on the grid, 22 are to get, 10 are gated, one is embargoed, one does not exist publicly and two are excluded; many of the to-get video sources are requests drafted in September 2026 and never sent.
- Key claims:
  - (reported) The registry holds one record per source, 60 in all, and its numbers are the records' own; the docs/research Markdown page comes from the same files.
  - (reported) Sample-level measured counts are empty for every record until a grid job runs on the owner's word.
  - (reported) C-ArmLSpine (149 lumbar intraoperative cases with CBCT) is the test set and the demo and was never trained on in the zero-shot arm; its level truth is projected from the CBCT with two labellers agreeing on 814 of 910 bodies, and it holds 3 real L6 cases.
  - (reported) FluoroCap's 29,500 literature panels are 72.8 percent reproductions with no pixel residual and are out of training by directive D1, a test set at most.
  - (reported) No public set of real cervical or thoracic C-arm frames with named levels exists; one's own captures with a preoperative CT are the way.
  - (reported) The two C-ArmLSpine CBCT projection sweeps (case134, case39) are the only confirmed consecutive-frame sequences with a paired volume, rotational rather than cranio-caudal.
  - (reported) NHANES II unannotated films are unused by a written criterion because pseudo-labels cut anomalous accuracy 20 points.
  - (reported) The NHANES II annotated films were counted twice in the old fold banks, a leak the registry ends.
  - (reported) VinDr-SpineXR sits on the grid under a PhysioNet agreement outside IRB determination 2026-269 with its own determination pending, was dropped from training, and only its implant boxes are used.
  - (reported) No weight trained on BUU-LSPINE leaves the grid until the owner has read its licence.
  - (reported) All 692 public COBRA2026 cases are on Ceph (808.615 GB); 145 Elekta cases are in a bank of 24 frames each, the rest never exported; the Varian geometry was solved 2026-09-23 but no bank built.
  - (reported) The seven Stanford, RSNA, CT-RATE, CADS and MAGIC-CT collections are gated behind data use agreements, none signed; the ungated CT-RATE re-uploads were deliberately not used.

### 6. OR Readiness Ledger

- Folder: `SchwingNet/artifacts/or-readiness-ledger`; account: first account; link id: TUNtAawdAQh2vvRT9kWNf2; page, ledger; date: 2026-09-22 (Header line: "Fluoroscopic level identification . adversarial readiness program . 2026-09-22 06:10 EDT"; a later re-s...); confidentiality: schwingnet-method.
- Question: Can SchwingNet's fluoroscopic level identification count vertebrae in an operating room (a C-arm device) it has never seen, and what did the overnight adversarial readiness program measure, refute and leave running?
- Summary: A page built at 06:10 EDT on 2026-09-22 from files read on the grid under or_ready/, reporting an overnight program of audits on SchwingNet's level identification. On the device it was trained on, level naming on real OR laterals reaches 98.2% exact level whole-frame (five-fold CV over 148 C-ArmLSpine cases, 95% CI 95.7 to 100), 92.9% with three vertebrae in field and 72.8% with one, with zero leakage pairs found against 37,165 films, 29,484 FluoroCap panels and 1,445 yolo_v6f images. On a device held out entirely the detector finds 83.9% of sacra and 90.6% of vertebrae on whole frames once truth boxes share one convention; the 5.2% in the overnight table was vertebral-body boxes scored against whole-vertebra outline predictions, and the re-score at 06:50 fixed it, while magnified three- and one-vertebra crops stay at 36% and 19%. The page rules the 100% DeepFluoro sacrum number not the manuscript's method, since its render init saw 44.8% of the test specimen's poses and its device terms were fitted on that specimen's real frames; the clean render-only number is 88.5 to 91.3%. Corruption robustness (408 rows), cross-population naming on COBRA and DeepFluoro (a zero-shot level model at 89 to 90% beats the C-ArmLSpine fine-tune's 43.5%), thoracic detection on 608 AASCE films (97 to 99% per level), a SpineSAC six-C-arm sawbone sheet with no truth, device cards for physics styling (styled frames not yet inside the real domain), and a status table of done, running, dead and never-started tracks follow, ending with what the evidence supports, what it does not (counting-grade detection on a fully held-out device, C2 on real images, the styling claim) and next steps with likelihood and impact.
- Key claims:
  - (measured) On the device it was trained on, level naming on real OR laterals is 98.2% exact whole-frame, cross-validated, with truth independent of the model and no leakage found.
  - (measured) No test frame is in any training corpus: zero near-duplicate pairs against every external corpus, splits disjoint by case.
  - (measured) A detector that never saw the C-ArmLSpine C-arm finds 91% of its vertebrae and 84% of its sacra on whole frames; the overnight 5.2% was a box-convention artefact.
  - (measured) Held-out recall collapses on magnified crops: 36% on a three-vertebra field (64% at conf 0.05) and 19% on one vertebra, while the same-device control keeps 82%; scale is a device-transfer lever of its own.
  - (measured) The 100% DeepFluoro sacrum number is not the manuscript's method: the same-device detector was initialised from a render model whose renders were 44.8% under the test specimen's poses and whose device terms were fitted on its real pixels.
  - (measured) Sacrum found on C-ArmLSpine is partly a statement about layout: a fixed median box hits the truth sacrum on 85.7% of test frames; vertebra recall and level accuracy are not explained this way.
  - (measured) The pipeline degrades gracefully under quantum noise to 300 photons, read noise to sigma 0.06, rotation to 15 degrees, and any gamma, magnification, obliquity, collimation, shutter or instrument in the tested range, costing under 10% of sacrum recall; inversion and a 180 degree flip break every detector at once.
  - (measured) A level model that never saw a device names 89 to 90% of COBRA lateral levels exactly, and fine-tuning the level model on one device costs it the others (43.5% versus 79.9% with oracle boxes).
  - (measured) Sacrum-anchored counting reaches 80 to 84% on a held-out device, and applies to DeepFluoro's AP pelvis frames where the crop prototype does not.
  - (measured) C2 on a real image is not demonstrated in any arm: the real-trained arm finds 0 to 8% of 25 labelled C2 instances; render-trained arms find 85 to 88% on renders and none on film.
  - (measured) Thoracic detection on real radiographs is at counting grade per level (97 to 99%) with the film keypoint detector; chains break on L5 and on one missed mid-thoracic body.
  - (measured) Counting-grade (97% or better) detection on a device held out entirely is not supported yet.
- Numbers (93 recorded; the first 10 here):
  - 98.2 % exact level, n = 148 cases, five-fold CV, interval 95.7-100 (95% CI): Whole C-ArmLSpine lateral frames, same device
  - 92.9 % exact level, interval 89.6-95.6 (95% CI): Lateral, three vertebrae in field
  - 72.8 % exact level: Lateral, one vertebra in field
  - 97.0 % exact level: Lateral, five vertebrae in field
  - 96.7 % exact level: AP whole frame
  - 86.3 % exact level: AP, three vertebrae
  - 37.9 % exact level: AP zero-shot level model (no C-ArmLSpine training), one-level cranial shift
  - 96.3 % exact level: Zero-shot lateral, whole frame
  - 0 near-duplicate pairs (NCC >= 0.97), n = 37,165 film images + 29,484 FluoroCap panels + 1,445 yolo_v6f: Real test frames against every external corpus
  - 105/15/28; 110/8/27; 4/1/1 cases or specimens per split: C-ArmLSpine, COBRA, DeepFluoro train/val/test split sizes; 0 cases in more than one split

### 7. Counting Levels Under Fluoroscopy

- Folder: `SchwingNet/artifacts/counting-levels-under-fluoroscopy`; account: first account; link id: 8uhYki72Jf355qdfLM5N96; page, proposal; date: 2026-09 (Header chip: "Proposal . intraoperative level counting . September 2026". The latest dated item is the run stamp: "Ev...); confidentiality: mixed.
- Question: Can the vertebral level be counted under C-arm fluoroscopy without a tracker, how exact is the count given correct boxes, where does synthetic training data stop, and what would retrospective in-house fluoroscopy let the method guarantee?
- Summary: A proposal prepared for a department spine surgeon and the department: count the level from an anchor the surgeon trusts (sacrum or C2) along a C-arm sweep, with the only learned stage a detector of vertebral bodies and the anchor, frames linked by normalised cross-correlation into a chain, and the target read as the k-th node; no name such as L4 is ever formed. On public CT renders and real radiographs the count is exact on 99.17 to 100% of walks across twelve arms (16 to 801 spines each), and the film-trained detector reaches 99.14% per-body recall on real films against 71.67% for a renders-only one. Synthetic fluoroscopy from CT was calibrated on DeepFluoro (366 frames, six cadaveric pelves): physics explains 81% (86% once the log-compressed display is read correctly) of a real frame's variance and the calibrated renderer 93.9% in attenuation space, yet a detector trained on living whole-body CT renders finds the sacrum on only 64 to 77% of real frames against 90 to 97% when trained on the test population's own CTs, and learned refiners add eight to ten points inside a 26-point seed spread. The proposal therefore asks for five years of the surgeon's own PACS fluoroscopy (2021 to 2026) under a retrospective protocol, annotation of a subset by a resident, and a measured bound on wrong-level output at a chosen decline rate, plus an immediate start on the WSU C-arm with a phantom and a cadaver. Wrong-level surgery is framed as 1 in 830 operations and 81% of events a count gone wrong under fluoroscopy.
- Key claims:
  - (reported) Wrong-level spine surgery occurs in 1 in 830 operations (1 in 240 thoracic) in a 34,544-case registry, half of surveyed spine surgeons have done it, and 81% of events are a count gone wrong under fluoroscopy.
  - (measured) The method is exact on 99.2 to 100% of walks when its boxes are right, so deployment is a detection question.
  - (inferred) The only learned component finds vertebral bodies and the anchor; nothing learned decides the level, and no name is formed.
  - (measured) Consecutive frames are linked by normalised cross-correlation restricted to within 0.6 of a body of the commanded step.
  - (measured) Twelve arms of 16 to 801 spines lie between 99.17 and 100% exactness; exactness is regional (99.7% lumbar, 95.5% thoracic, 83.8% cervical).
  - (measured) The film-trained detector reaches 99.14% per-body recall on real film; the same architecture trained on renders only reaches 71.67%.
  - (measured) Exact geometry and polyenergetic attenuation explain 81% of a real DeepFluoro frame's variance; calibrated device terms and a per-frame smooth field bring it to 93.9% against a 99.5% quantum-noise ceiling.
  - (measured) A detector trained on renders of DeepFluoro's own CTs finds the sacrum on 90 to 97% of real frames; trained on renders of living whole-body CTs, 64 to 77%; the population, not the renderer, limits it.
  - (measured) Learned refiners add eight to ten points, all smaller than the 26-point seed spread and none within fifteen points of the population-matched control.
  - (measured) The refined render agrees with the real frame slightly less than the plain calibrated one (68.1% vs 69.3% of display variance), better on one frame in five.
  - (measured) The collimator border ring, a fifth of the field, holds about two thirds of the residual; the earlier thin-bone deficit was that border and has been withdrawn.
  - (measured) The stored DeepFluoro frames are log-compressed display images; read correctly, physics explains 86% rather than 80% and the calibrated renderer about 90% of a held-out frame.
- Numbers (59 recorded; the first 10 here):
  - 1 in 830 operations, n = 34,544-case registry: wrong-level spine surgery rate
  - 1 in 240 operations, n = 34,544-case registry: wrong-level rate in the thoracic spine
  - half of surveyed spine surgeons: surgeons who have done a wrong-level operation once
  - 81 % of events: wrong-level events that are a count gone wrong under fluoroscopy
  - 99.2-100 % of walks exact: count exactness when the detector's boxes are right
  - about ten operating-room minutes per case: the priceable saving
  - $140k-$500k dollars per year, n = 300-1,000 cases a year: priced operating-room minutes
  - 0.6 of a body: restriction on the cross-correlation match relative to the commanded step
  - 100 % exact, n = 834 / 834 walks, 76 spines: from C2, three bodies of overlap, whole-spine CT
  - 99.67 % exact, n = 1,205 / 1,209 walks, 262 spines: from C2, real lateral radiographs
- Datasets: DeepFluoro (366 real C-arm frames, six cadaveric pelves, Siemens Cios Fusion, with CT and pose), public labelled CT renders (whole-spine and abdominopelvic CT, unnamed), real lateral radiographs and annotated real plain films (unnamed), second-institution spines (unnamed), living whole-body CTs (unnamed), proposed: retrospective PACS fluoroscopy from spine cases 2021-2026; tools: normalised cross-correlation linker, tools/deepfluoro_anchor_report.py, DeepDRR, SyntheX, RealDRR-style refiner, Fourier low-band swap, lossless frame grabber, anthropomorphic lumbar spine phantom, Siemens Cios Fusion C-arm, WSU C-arm fluoroscope (0312 Elliman); models: the vertebral body and anchor detector (film-trained; renders-only variant), RealDRR-style refiner, high-pass-only refiner.
- Decisions:
  - Count, not name: the method asserts 'four from the sacrum', never 'L4'; nothing learned decides the level., the proposal's author: the count is exact wherever the boxes are right, so deployment becomes a detection question; transitional anatomy is counted as it is operated on
  - The earlier reported thin-bone deficit is withdrawn., the proposal's author: it was the collimator border counted as a bone edge
  - Refusal, not a guess: when a frame fails to link or the anchor is not found, the walk is declined., the proposal's author: a decline is a repeat exposure, not a wrong level; wrong answers are converted into declines at a rate the operator chooses
  - The retrospective test set is held out by case, never by frame., the proposal's author
  - Start this week on the WSU C-arm with a phantom, then a cadaver, before patient data., the proposal's author: operator-side feasibility does not need patient data, and the paperwork's fifteen-business-day lead time is the critical path
- Open questions: The per-case wrong-level rate on the department's own patients and machines, which no cadaver or synthetic data can predict. / Whether a person can execute the sweep on a real C-arm and whether the linker holds on real detector output. / The decline rate at each operating point on held-out cases. / Whether the renderer's DeepFluoro calibration transfers to the WSU machine's frames (one C-arm session with a step wedge and pose phantom is asked for).

### 8. Counting From The Sacrum

- Folder: `SchwingNet/artifacts/counting-from-the-sacrum`; account: first account; link id: WSW2UJAR4ar2PjmbymLVRf; page, report; date: 2026-09-22 (Header line 'Wayne State . 22 September 2026' and the section 'What today's audit found'.); confidentiality: schwingnet-method.
- Question: Does naming the vertebral level from C-arm exposures alone, by counting from the sacrum with no CT, tracker or registration, beat the published literature, and what did the 22 September 2026 audit of the synthetic-data pipeline find?
- Summary: A one-page report of the registration-free level-naming method (expose, detect, link, chain, count, confirm; only the shaded boxes learned) with the figure fig1_story.png, stating that every number is verified against a file on disk (docs/RSNAI_CLAIMS.json, 54 claims). On naming it claims a wide lead: 98.22% exact level on real intraoperative lateral C-arm frames (141 cases, 617 vertebrae, 5-fold CV; 100% within one; 95% case-bootstrap lower bound 95.71%), 96.67% AP, 96.27% zero-shot lateral with no C-ArmLSpine data in training, and 99.87% (n 3,720) on CSXA cervical and 98.04% (n 2,698) on BUU-LSPINE plain radiographs, against a best published registration-free 73.3% that imports identity from a preoperative radiograph and LevelCheck, which needs the patient's CT. On detection it concedes the binding constraint: 90.6% per-body recall on a held-out device against published 0.96-0.98, and a render-only detector at 71.7% on real radiographs versus 99.1% trained on real film. The audit found the C-arm cards do sample fitted distributions (51 of 114 device-parameter pairs from a Gaussian copula conditioned on water-equivalent thickness), but a clip on 8-11 correlated terms saturates rather than truncates, so renders reached only 1.7-5.1% of the joint with 94.9-98.3% of frames carrying a pinned term; a replacement sampler reaching 99% is implemented but not adopted pending an A/B. The CT axis was re-counted by distinct subject (TotalSegmentator v3 1,735, v2 1,170, CTSpinoPelvic1K 801, VerSe 374, C-ArmLSpine 120). Open: whether a CTSpinoPelvic1K release id is one person (prone and supine colonography), a fleet-wide detector training now (18,906 frames, 56 test specimens), and 2,654 frames of vendor kilovoltage on disk unused.
- Key claims:
  - (measured) On real intraoperative lateral C-arm frames the method names the exact level in 98.22% of vertebrae (141 cases, 617 vertebrae, 5-fold CV) and is within one level 100% of the time.
  - (measured) The 95% case-bootstrap confidence interval on the lateral whole-frame figure has a lower bound of 95.71%; a naive interval would cover only 70.8% of the time because frames within a case are not independent.
  - (reported) The best published registration-free naming is 73.3% and imports identity from a preoperative radiograph; LevelCheck registers to the patient's own CT and is a ceiling, not a rival.
  - (measured) Zero-shot, with no C-ArmLSpine data in training, the method reaches 96.27% exact on lateral and 37.88% on AP.
  - (measured) On plain radiographs held out by patient the method reaches 99.87% on CSXA cervical (n 3,720) and 98.04% on BUU-LSPINE lumbar (n 2,698), 94.89% with only three vertebrae visible.
  - (measured) Detection, not naming, is the binding constraint: per-body recall on a fully held-out device is 90.6% (IoU 0.5) against published class-agnostic C-arm detection of 0.96-0.98, and recall compounds over eight to twelve bodies.
  - (measured) A detector trained on 6,650 renders and no real image gets 71.7% on real radiographs against 99.1% for the same architecture trained on real film, a 28-point sim-to-real tax matching the field.
  - (measured) Of 114 device-parameter pairs, 51 are drawn per frame from a fitted Gaussian copula conditioned on water-equivalent thickness and 9 come from DICOM headers; it is distribution sampling, not noise fitted to reproduce images.
  - (measured) A clip applied to 8-11 correlated terms saturates rather than truncates, so renders reached only 1.7-5.1% of the joint distribution and 94.9-98.3% of frames carry a pinned term.
  - (measured) The replacement sampler keeps every marginal, rejects only combinations a device never produces by the same typicality statistic, reaches 99% of the joint, and is implemented and tested but not adopted pending a running A/B.
  - (inferred) The saturation was found by looking at the figure (stacked spikes in the clipped kilovoltage histogram), not the numbers; the second time rendering settled what reasoning from numbers did not.
  - (measured) The benchmark's CT axis had named 802 abdominopelvic records; it now names every collection by distinct subject with the split's keying: TotalSegmentator v3 1,735, v2 1,170, CTSpinoPelvic1K 801, VerSe 374, C-ArmLSpine 120.
- Numbers (49 recorded; the first 10 here):
  - 98.2 % exact level, n = 141 cases, 617 vertebrae, interval 95% case-bootstrap CI lower bound 95.71%: Real intraoperative lateral C-arm frames, whole frame, 5-fold CV
  - 98.22 % exact level, n = 141 cases, 617 vertebrae, 5-fold CV, interval lower bound 95.71% (95% case-bootstrap): Lateral, whole frame
  - 100 % within one level, n = 617 vertebrae: Lateral, whole frame
  - 96.67 % exact level: AP, whole frame (within one 100%)
  - 97.00 % exact level: Lateral, five vertebrae in the field
  - 92.86 % exact level: Lateral, three vertebrae in the field
  - 72.85 % exact level: Lateral, one vertebra in the field (within one 90.23%)
  - 90.23 % within one level: Lateral, one vertebra in the field
  - 96.27 % exact level: Zero-shot lateral, no C-ArmLSpine data in training
  - 37.88 % exact level: Zero-shot AP
- Datasets: C-ArmLSpine (141 cases, 617 vertebrae; 120 distinct subjects), CSXA cervical (3,720 test windows), BUU-LSPINE lumbar (2,698 test windows), TotalSegmentator v3 and v2, CTSpinoPelvic1K (from CT colonography, prone and supine), VerSe, DICOM headers and vendor exposure files of the C-arm acquisitions, renders (6,650) from the C-arm device cards; tools: fitted Gaussian copula sampler conditioned on water-equivalent thickness, typicality statistic / typicality gate, case-bootstrap confidence intervals, 5-fold CV, docs/RSNAI_CLAIMS.json, docs/DEVICE_PARAM_AUDIT.md, docs/DEVICE_SPREAD_SWEEP.md, paper/rsnai/figures/fig1_story.png (seeded, reproducing runs), C-arm device cards (Philips BV Pulsera, Siemens Cios Fusion, Elekta XVI, Trixell Pixium 4600, Surgio B51 DR, Varian Halcyon), split list builder that refuses to write a subject or specimen on both sides; models: the level-naming chain: expose, detect, link, chain, count, confirm (only the shaded boxes learned), class-agnostic vertebra detector, held-out-device and render-only variants, single fleet-wide detector over every C-arm (training now), LevelCheck (comparator: registers to the patient's own CT).
- Decisions:
  - The replacement sampler is implemented and tested but not adopted; the running A/B decides. (2026-09-22): A sampler that reaches more of the distribution is not automatically better training data.
  - Every confidence interval is clustered at the case. (2026-09-22): Frames within a case are not independent; a naive interval covers 70.8% of the time.
  - The CT axis names every collection by distinct subject, counted with the same keying the train/validation split uses. (2026-09-22): The benchmark had been scored on 802 abdominopelvic records, which see only the lower spine.
  - The fleet-wide detector's list builder refuses to write anything if a subject or specimen lands on both sides of the split. (2026-09-22)
  - Both the naming result and the unsolved detection problem are reported. (2026-09-22): Reporting both is what makes the first one credible.
  - The CTSpinoPelvic1K id-is-a-person question is not assumed fine; an audit is running. (2026-09-22): A mis-split would be larger than either data leak already found and fixed.
- Open questions: Whether a CTSpinoPelvic1K release id is one human being (prone and supine colonography scans), which would mis-split the corpus. / The result of the single fleet-wide detector training now (18,906 frames, 56 test specimens). / Whether the replacement sampler improves training data (the A/B is running). / Using the 2,654 frames of vendor kilovoltage that one card records as unavailable. / Row (d) of the figure is still rendering.

### 9. Vertebral Count Anomaly Rule

- Folder: `SchwingNet/artifacts/vertebral-count-anomaly-rule`; account: first account; link id: KkUJQLF5f9MZd8m8YCbRnf; design, visual-abstract; date: 2026-09-22 (canvas.json: "createdOnFiles": { "v": 1, "at": "2026-09-22T20:40:00Z" }; the design page itself carries no date.); confidentiality: schwingnet-method.
- Question: How does the counting rule R + C = 22 flag a vertebral column with an extra or missing vertebra, and what can counting alone not tell?
- Summary: An interactive design (one board, R + C = 22) that explains the vertebral count anomaly rule: C1 is excluded from these datasets, so an ordinary column holds 23 vertebrae (C2 to C7, T1 to T12, L1 to L5), and for any target vertebra the count above it (R, rostral) plus the count below it (C, caudal) must be 22, with the sacrum as the anchor the count starts from. Clicking a vertebra moves the target; three variants can be chosen, the ordinary column of 23, a sixth lumbar (24) and a thirteenth thoracic (24), and the page shows R, C, their sum against the expected 22, a verdict (ordinary column or anomaly detected) and what the report would say: with 22 the level is reported by name, otherwise the ordinal from C2 and from the sacrum is reported and the name withheld, since a transitional column cannot be named by counting alone. It states the rule's limits: it counts and nothing more, cannot say which vertebra is extra so flags the column not the level, passes a column with both an extra and a missing vertebra, and compounds the counting error over the whole column, so one missed body anywhere flags an ordinary spine.
- Key claims:
  - (inferred) With C1 excluded, an ordinary column holds 23 vertebrae and for any target the count above plus the count below must be 22.
  - (inferred) When the sum is not 22 the ordinal is reported and the name withheld, because a transitional column cannot be named by counting alone.
  - (inferred) The rule cannot say which vertebra is the extra one, so it flags the column rather than the level.
  - (inferred) A column carrying both an extra and a missing vertebra sums to 22 and passes.
  - (inferred) The rule's error is the counting error compounded over the whole column: one missed body anywhere flags an ordinary spine.
- Numbers (4 recorded; the first 4 here):
  - 23 vertebrae in an ordinary column: C2 to C7, T1 to T12, L1 to L5 with C1 excluded
  - 22 expected sum R + C: Count above the target plus count below it in an ordinary column
  - 24 vertebrae: Column total for the sixth-lumbar and thirteenth-thoracic variants
  - 1180 x 900 pixels: Size of the interactive board in canvas.json
- Datasets: 'these datasets' (unnamed) from which C1 is excluded; tools: an interactive Design artifact (project/Main.dc.html with canvas.json, one board titled R + C = 22); models: none named.
- Decisions:
  - C1 is excluded from the data and from the arithmetic.: C1 is excluded from these datasets
  - When R + C is not 22, the ordinal from C2 and from the sacrum is reported and the level name is withheld.: a transitional column cannot be named by counting alone
  - The sacrum is the anchor the count starts from.

### 10. Level Counting Visual Abstract

- Folder: `SchwingNet/artifacts/level-counting-visual-abstract`; account: first account; link id: CwxKcL5qQt2Pv37u56ndst; page, visual-abstract; date: 2026 (No header date; the latest dates mentioned are 2026 citations (Furuya 2026, Bettis 2026) and the cost model's 'x 1.35...); confidentiality: mixed.
- Question: Why count the vertebral level from the sacrum on the C-arm a hospital already owns, and what does wrong-level surgery cost?
- Summary: A one-page visual abstract for spine surgeons and a hospital CFO. It frames wrong-level spine surgery as rare per operation and common per career (1 in 830 operations in a 34,544-case registry, half of surveyed surgeons), almost always a counting error under fluoroscopy, and navigation suites as too costly for a problem this rare. The method is drawn as five steps: expose at the anchor, detect bodies plus anchor (the only learned step), link overlapping frames, count k from the anchor, confirm with the instrument on bone; no tracker, no reference frame, no capital purchase. Stat tiles give operating-room time (15 to 3 minutes with a fiducial), indemnity ($75k median), navigation list price (about $1 to 2.5M, labelled an assumption) and unmeasured peace of mind, and a per-hospital model prices events and localization minutes for 300, 600 and 1,000 cases a year with every input labelled. The manuscript in preparation reports exact counts on 99.17 to 100% of reached walks with the annotation's boxes and 1,205 of 1,209 on 262 real lateral radiographs; detection is the deployment risk, which a retrospective fluoroscopy collection is asked for to measure.
- Key claims:
  - (reported) Wrong-level surgery occurs in 1 in 830 operations and half of surveyed surgeons have done it once.
  - (reported) Most wrong-level events are intraoperative disorientation, a count that went wrong.
  - (unverified) The method needs no tracker, reference frame or capital purchase: one learned detector and an exact count on the existing C-arm.
  - (measured) With correct boxes the count is exact on 99.17 to 100% of walks.
  - (measured) On 262 real lateral radiographs from C2, 1,205 of 1,209 were counted correctly.
  - (inferred) Deployment risk is detection, not counting.
  - (unverified) Navigation suites address screw placement and no study shows they reduce wrong-level surgery.
  - (inferred) The priceable savings are operating-room minutes; the never event is a harm, not a line item.
- Numbers (23 recorded; the first 10 here):
  - 1 in 830 operations, n = 34,544 cases: Wrong-level rate in the Furuya 2026 registry, 0.12%
  - 1 in 240 operations: Thoracic wrong-level rate, 0.42%
  - half of surveyed surgeons: Surgeons who have done a wrong-level operation once (Mody 2008)
  - 81 % of events: Intraoperative disorientation as the cause
  - 15 -> 3 minutes, n = 19 vs 11; 26 vs 26 patients: Thoracic localization by needle-and-count versus pre-placed fiducial, two small series
  - $34-37 per OR minute (2014 dollars): Before physician fees; 30 to 40% more today (Childers 2018)
  - $1,750-1,950 dollars: Cost of a pre-placed fiducial, plus a second visit
  - $75k median indemnity, interval IQR $29k-$215k: Per paid surgical never-event claim (Bettis 2026, NPDB)
  - 17 % of events: Self-reported wrong-level events that led to legal action
  - 81 % of litigated cases: Wrong-disc-level cases ending in a payment (Goodkin 2004)
- Datasets: a 34,544-case degenerative spine registry (Furuya 2026), NPDB 1991-2025 malpractice payments, Joint Commission Sentinel Event Data 2024, 262 real lateral radiographs, twelve arms of 16 to 801 spines; tools: the C-arm the hospital already owns, a vertebra detector and a counter; models: one learned detector (vertebra bodies plus anchor).
- Decisions:
  - We ask for our own fluoroscopy, retrospectively, to measure performance on our machines and patients., the authors: how it performs on our machines and our patients is the one number none of this can predict
  - Navigation's capital figure is modelled as an assumption ($1.5M over 7 years plus 10% service) and labelled as such., the authors: no verified capital figure exists in the literature
- Open questions: How the method performs on our machines and our patients (detection on real fluoroscopy). / Navigation penetration by site of care, unreported. / Surgeon workload or satisfaction with versus without navigation, unmeasured.
- Stale markers: The manuscript in preparation / 2014 dollars, 30-40% more today / x 1.35 (to 2026) / Joint Commission Sentinel Event Data 2024 / NPDB 1991-2025

- Coverage: One HTML page, read whole; no images. The five-step flow was read from arrow-separated text. About five lesser numbers left out (the $1.5M and 7-year amortisation inputs repeated, 'nearly one in five'). No instruction-like text.

### 11. What Is Left After Physics

- Folder: `SchwingNet/artifacts/what-is-left-after-physics`; account: first account; link id: AL3mndDMgZGLGDGxJ87ghR; page, analysis; date: 2026 (No header date or stamp; the latest date mentioned is 'The 2026 diffusion paper, read directly' (Rapuri et al., arXiv...); confidentiality: schwingnet-method.
- Question: After a calibrated physics render of a specimen's own CT at the real C-arm pose, what residual is left in real DeepFluoro fluoroscopy frames, and could a learned CT-to-fluoroscopy model supply it?
- Summary: Four real DeepFluoro frames are decomposed against renders of their own CT at the same pose (geometry checked to 0.00 px): the calibrated render alone explains 54.6 to 90.1% of attenuation variance, and a twelve-coefficient smooth field (window, scatter pedestal, heel, vignette) lifts that to 86.9 to 97.1%, so the smooth field is most of the gap. After the field the 2 to 16 px bands sit at 0.1 to 0.8% of variance, two to seven times the quantum noise floor, so the claim that a learned model would have 'nothing local' to learn was too strong; the residual is cortical edges sharper than a 1 mm CT magnified 1.5x onto 0.19 mm pixels can give, plus bowel gas that moved. The smooth field is learnable but not predictable from the CT; sharpness is only obtainable by inventing it; the bowel is unknowable. A verified literature pass (105 claims, 25 checked, 10 survived) finds the same low-frequency per-image residual in FDA, ultrasound sim-to-real, SyntheX, DeepDRR, N4 bias-field and BatchNorm work, and the 2026 diffusion paper's Table 1 shows physics DRRs with pose augmentation beating a generator at every threshold. The page ends with three direct tests to run next, read with seeds averaged and specimens as folds, because every published effect is smaller than the detector's 26-point seed spread.
- Key claims:
  - (measured) The smooth per-frame field is most of the sim-to-real gap: it takes one frame from 83.0 to 91.6% explained and the worst specimen from 54.6 to 86.9%.
  - (measured) 'Nothing local' was too strong: after the field the 2 to 16 px bands are two to seven times the noise floor.
  - (inferred) The remaining edge lines come from the real frame being sharper than the CT can render; the bowel mottle is gas that moved between CT and fluoroscopy.
  - (inferred) A network can learn the smooth field from paired data but cannot predict it from the CT, since the device sets it per exposure.
  - (inferred) Cortical sharpness below CT resolution can only be invented, and no paired-DRR paper evaluated invented texture on a downstream task.
  - (reported) Fourier Domain Adaptation gains the same from an 11x11-bin band as from nine times that width, so the transferable gap lives in the smallest band.
  - (reported) Ultrasound sim-to-real moved Dice 43.2 to 46.7 by swapping only DC plus the first harmonic.
  - (reported) SyntheX on the same six DeepFluoro specimens reached parity with real-frame training using physics renders plus strong randomisation, and CycleGAN added nothing under strong randomisation.
  - (reported) DeepDRR's physics fidelity moved landmark error from 78.2 mm (naive) to 24.7 mm (full model); scatter moved only 26.9 to 24.7.
  - (reported) Removing the MRI bias field before nnU-Net gave no gain and one significant loss; under cross-vendor shift the lever was feature normalisation (InstanceNorm to BatchNorm, 0.63 to 0.80 Dice).
  - (reported) In the 2026 diffusion paper, DRRs with +/-2.5 degrees view augmentation were the best arm at every threshold; a generator wins only where the detector is already confident.
  - (inferred) 'Learning to project' as a deterministic map from the CT is impossible in principle, and as a learned distribution it is what randomisation already is.
- Numbers (44 recorded; the first 10 here):
  - 366 real C-arm frames: DeepFluoro real frames in the corpus
  - 4 frames: real frames decomposed on the page
  - 0.00 px: geometry check between real frame and render pose
  - 9 device terms: calibrated device terms in the renderer
  - 12 coefficients: quadratic smooth field per frame
  - 98th percentile: symmetric colour scale per row
  - 83.0 / 91.6 % variance explained: frame 17-1882/000 (near AP): render alone / plus smooth field
  - 0.42; 0.31; 0.30; 0.45; 0.83 % of variance: 17-1882/000: noise floor; 2-4, 4-8, 8-16, >32 px bands
  - 80.1 / 87.1 % variance explained: frame 17-1882/050: render alone / plus smooth field
  - 0.12; 0.32; 0.54; 0.80; 1.71 % of variance: 17-1882/050: noise floor; 2-4, 4-8, 8-16, >32 px bands
- Datasets: DeepFluoro (366 real C-arm frames, cadaveric pelvis, six specimens), GTA5 and Cityscapes (FDA benchmark), M&Ms challenge cardiac data (Full et al. 2020), three glioma MRI datasets (Kondrateva et al., unnamed), Field II synthetic and real B-mode ultrasound (Sharifzadeh et al.); tools: calibrated physics renderer (polyenergetic attenuation, nine device terms), DeepDRR, SyntheX, Fourier Domain Adaptation (FDA), N4 bias-field correction, nnU-Net, CycleGAN, ADDA, CLAHE and histogram matching (noted as absent from the record); models: the current vertebra detector (YOLO11, BatchNorm), a 2-D diffusion generator (Rapuri et al. 2026), CycleGAN, ADDA, nnU-Net.
- Decisions:
  - Three direct tests are next: sample the fitted 12-coefficient surfaces of the 366 real frames as an augmentation on renders; correlate missed frames with their field coefficients and view angle; fit-and-divide-out the surface on each real frame at inference.: The residual decomposition is measured in pixel variance and nothing in it connects to the detector's misses.
  - All three tests are read with seeds averaged and specimens as folds.: Every effect size in the literature is smaller than the 26-point seed spread this detector shows at one seed.
  - Only claims that survived verification are reported; the rest is listed as absent.: A verified literature pass: 105 claims, 25 adversarially checked, 10 survived.

### 12. Ninety-Three Pixels

- Folder: `SchwingNet/artifacts/ninety-three-pixels`; account: first account; link id: KXtF9YQpjYmfBtw81yLXEX; page, board; date: 2026-09-21 (Header chip '21 Sep 2026, 03:3x EDT grid [the owner's login], branch L6_Class, 8 GPU jobs live'.); confidentiality: schwingnet-method.
- Question: Does the coarse pixel spacing of the render corpora (93 pixels for a 140 mm field) explain the detector's failures, and can the DRR renderer be calibrated against real fluoroscopy using DeepFluoro?
- Summary: An overnight status board on the intraoperative level-counting work. It measures that the render corpora are 4 to 15 times coarser than a C-arm intensifier (ts_drr gives 93 px for a 140 mm field) but that a downsampling ladder on real films shows 836 px to 139 px costs 0.00 points of per-vertebra recall, so resolution is not the main event and the C2 failure needs another explanation. DeepFluoro (366 real frames, 6 cadaveric specimens, exact poses) was unblocked by presenting Chrome's TLS fingerprint, read to its geometry (reprojection 0.00 px median over 111 frames), and used to calibrate an image-formation model: physics alone explains 80.9% of attenuation variance, nine device terms add +4.3 points held out, the ceiling is 99.5%, and the residual has nothing local to learn, so the map was calibrated, not learned, and the GAN route was refuted by the literature. A two-class detector trained on 597 calibrated synthetic frames finds the sacrum on 76.9% of 365 real frames, with a 26-point seed spread bounding every arm comparison; the C2 arm's number is void (6 correct of 2593 walks) because C2 is inside a sweep frame only 15% of the time. Eight domain-randomization training arms are live, several silent failures were caught (a holdout bank deleted, films washed white, contamination checks erring toward clean), and the decision on the table is whether to re-render at C-arm resolution or price the resolution term first.
- Key claims:
  - (measured) The render corpora are 4 to 15 times too coarse against a C-arm intensifier.
  - (measured) Coarse fields cost nothing in per-vertebra recall on real films.
  - (measured) The physics renderer already explains 80.9% of a real frame's attenuation variance.
  - (measured) Nine device terms add about four points held out and transfer because every term belongs to the device.
  - (measured) The CT-to-fluoro map should be calibrated, not learned: the residual is a smooth per-frame field with nothing local.
  - (reported) A DRR-to-fluoro GAN buys nothing according to the literature and was not built.
  - (measured) A detector trained on calibrated synthetic frames alone finds the sacrum on 76.9% of real DeepFluoro frames.
  - (measured) The training seed alone moves sacrum recall by 26 points, so single-seed arms cannot be ranked.
  - (measured) 160 of 366 DeepFluoro frames are stored upside down; fixing the writer re-scored every arm.
  - (measured) The C2 arm's number is void because C2 is inside a sweep frame only 15% of the time.
  - (measured) The JHU archive's 403 was the TLS fingerprint; a Chrome-impersonating client is served normally.
  - (reported) No published per-vertebra recall on real intraoperative fluoroscopy exists.
- Numbers (60 recorded; the first 10 here):
  - 80.9 % variance explained, n = 366 pairs, 6 specimens: Physics-only formation model against real fluoroscopy, no detector term; NCC 0.899
  - +4.3 points: Nine device terms held out, scatter boxed; AP to lateral +4.7
  - 76.9 % sacrum recall, n = 365 frames, six specimens, interval by specimen 47.8-96.2%: Two-class detector trained on calibrated synthetic only, tested on real DeepFluoro frames
  - 99.5 % ceiling: Noise-floor ceiling; 93.9% reached with a per-frame surface
  - 0.00 points recall: Cost of 93-px fields, measured on the downsampling ladder
  - 2.59 GB: DeepFluoro landed on the grid
  - 260 GB freed: Disk freed; 3966 of 4295 GB soft limit
  - 93 px: Height of the 140 mm field in ts_drr at 1.5 mm/px; verse_drr 140, verse_soft_drr 151, ctsp_drr 175, C-arm coarse 350, C-arm fine 1400
  - 1024 px: Resolution the YOLO detector was trained at
  - 99.14 / 99.15 / 99.57 / 99.14 % recall at 0.05: Ladder on real films at 1x (836 px), 2x (418), 3x (279), 6x (139); recall at 0.25: 98.71/99.15/99.14/98.28
- Datasets: DeepFluoro (ipcai_2020), TotalSegmentator, VerSe, FluoroCap, 11,007 labelled real films, ts_drr, verse_drr, verse_soft_drr, ctsp_drr, physics_drr, anchor_drr_c2, nhanes_drr, films_drr_buucorr, films_ap_drr, spinalai_drr, anchor_drr, fluorocap_drr, vindr_det_drr, vindr_pseudo_drr, mendeley_drr, aasce_drr, [another group's study folder]; tools: curl_cffi (impersonate chrome), tools/deepfluoro_anchor_report.py, tools/holdout.py, corpus_resolution.py, probe_first_field.py, probe_c2_lands_on.py, dr_eval, DeepDRR (referenced renderer), the grid ([the owner's login]), 8 GPU jobs; models: YOLO detector trained at 1024 px (film-trained), two-class detector (vertebra, sacrum) on calibrated synthetic frames, anchor_c2 v1 cranial detector, 9 classes, calibrated DRR image-formation model with nine device terms, eight domain-randomization arms (dr_s00f00 to v5f_p08), refuted: CycleGAN, ADDA, pix2pix, paired diffusion.

It enlarges the section 3 table: COBRA, C-ArmLSpine, DeepFluoro and Ljubljana gain mask and labeller notes (814 of 910 bodies agree), IPCAI 2025 moves from 'not yet, 5 CT, 6.7 GB' to fetched, 50 X-rays, 5 CT, 6.2 GB, femur, and th...
- Question: Can SchwingNet turn an X-ray in one position into another view, detect and classify vertebrae at any C-arm position, and use paired X-ray and CT sets as gold truth for the 3D prior, and what must the census count before each model is trained?
- Summary: A confidential plan (patent material, before the provisional) with three goals: a network that turns an X-ray in one position into another (AP to lateral), a detector and classifier that works at any position, and paired X-ray and CT sets as gold ground truth for the 3D prior. Its short answer grades them: paired X-ray and CT as gold truth is strong (LevelCheck labelled 100% of views at about 26 s and 99.3% at 7 s); any-position detection and naming is feasible with a measured gap on C-ArmLSpine (lateral naming 96.4% zero-shot, AP naming 43.7%, detection 0.3% from renders alone, 80.5% with other devices' real frames, 84.0% with 24 frames of the device); AP image to lateral image is hard as stated, so the plan is structured transfer of levels, corners and alignment with pixel synthesis only as an auxiliary task. Section 3 now tables nine paired sets (COBRA, C-ArmLSpine, DeepFluoro, Ljubljana, a digital-twin cadaver, LUMOS, Geneva hip phantom, IPCAI 2025, pig limb, Veriserum), with notes on which have masks and which are not spine, and states a goal of 22 October to segment every paired set. Section 4 now holds the census counts per region (run on 2026-10-22) beside the counts needed to test each approach; section 5 gives the order of work and says nothing appears in an external text before the provisional is filed.
- Key claims:
  - (measured) Lateral naming transfers from 3D renders: 96.4% zero-shot on C-ArmLSpine, within a point of the 97.4% fine-tuned on the device.
  - (measured) AP naming zero-shot collapses to 43.7%, a one-level cranial shift that is a counting error.
  - (measured) Detection does not transfer from renders (0.3%); other devices' real frames carry most of it (80.5%); 24 frames of the device give 84.0%.
  - (unverified) No published work turns a spine AP film into a lateral; the closest moves the view only 9 degrees, so a synthesised lateral is a display aid, never a film.
  - (reported) Registration-based level labelling is near perfect: LevelCheck 100% of views at about 26 s, 99.3% at 7 s.
  - (measured) The project holds more real AP-lateral pairs than assumed: BUU-LSPINE's 3,600 people, on the scale of LatXGen's 2,611 training pairs.
  - (measured) Every paired spine set, held or not, is lumbar or lumbosacral except one cadaver reaching T2; thoracic and cervical parts of the prior can be checked only on renders.
  - (reported) SyntheX shows physics renders can carry detection but not always, so the 0.3% may be the renderer's fault, which ablation #69 tests.
  - (inferred) About 100 test persons per region gives a 95% interval near +/-6 points at 90% accuracy and sets the count needed to test.
  - (measured) The census has run and its counts fill the per-region table: 140 groups, 7,008,826 files, 7.3 TB; only lumbar and lumbosacral regions hold X-ray with CT.
  - (inferred) The goal of 22 October is to segment every paired set so every real X-ray with a CT carries gold per-pixel labels.
  - (inferred) The first steps need no census; the decision to train each model waits for the per-region counts.

  - 97.4 % naming accuracy: lateral naming fine-tuned on the device
  - 43.7 % naming accuracy: AP naming on C-ArmLSpine, zero-shot
  - 0.3 % detection: detection on C-ArmLSpine from renders alone
  - 80.5 % detection: detection with other devices' real frames plus renders
  - 84.0 % detection, n = 24 frames of the device added: detection with 24 frames of the test device

  - ~25.4 PSNR: XraySyn at +/-9 degrees

- Decisions:

### 15. Connect the grid to GitHub

- Folder: `SchwingNet/artifacts/connect-the-grid-to-github`; account: first account; link id: JYgue8HkJJbuiNrsAUfhYF; docs, runbook; date: 2026-10-14 (Byline 'Oct 14, 2026 . @Someone' and the line 'You have Write access on spine-program already (checked 2026-10-14)'.); confidentiality: mixed.
- Question: How does a student's Beethoven session on the Wayne State grid obtain a GitHub login so it can clone the private spine-program repository and push to the student's own repositories, without the token ever reaching Claude, a job script or a repository?
- Summary: A four-step guide, about ten minutes each, written for a student whose Beethoven session on the Wayne State grid already clones public repositories from a compute node but lacks a login. Step 1 makes one classic personal access token with the repo scope (90-day expiry; a fine-grained token per resource owner is the fallback if the organization disallows classic tokens). Step 2 stores it in ~/.beethoven/git-credentials (mode 700 folder, 600 file) typed in an editor, points git's credential helper at it and sets the GitHub no-reply address for commits. Step 3 clones spine-program into the student's own folder on the lab volume, not under hpcworker, and checks that the clone asks for no username; it notes that results/, figures/, manuscripts/ and regulatory/ are links into a shared archive the owner has yet to fill, and points to issue 19 as a first task. Step 4 pushes to the student's own repositories, with the two-token credential.useHttpPath variant. A table of refusals (no credential file, repository not found, permission denied, invalid token, lab-volume permission, killed on the login node) closes it. Three rules frame it: the token is typed by the student alone, the Claude GitHub App links are for claude.ai sessions and are skipped, and commands run on a compute node since git is untested on the login node.
- Key claims:
  - (measured) The student's Beethoven session already reaches GitHub and cloned two public repositories from the compute node; it lacks only a login.
  - (measured) The student already has Write access on spine-program.
  - (inferred) One classic token with the repo scope covers spine-program and the student's own repositories at once.
  - (inferred) The token must never go on a command line, into a job script, into a chat with Claude or into any repository file; Claude on the grid must not see it.
  - (unverified) Commands run in the Beethoven session's terminal on a compute node, not the login node, where git has not been tested.
  - (inferred) A clone that prints Receiving objects and ends without asking for a username proves git found the token and GitHub accepted it.
  - (unverified) The clone goes in the student's own folder on the lab volume, not under hpcworker, whose run folders the framework empties.

### 16. State of the literature: datasets held, missing, size and power

- Folder: `SchwingNet/artifacts/state-of-the-literature-datasets-held-missing-size-and-power-8hg1ay`; account: second account; link id: 8Hg1AyjzCf7VhCjPAQw8sY; docs, analysis; date: 2026-10-08 (Header line 'Oct 8, 2026 . @[the owner]'.); confidentiality: schwingnet-other.
- Question: Which datasets of the field are held or missing, and how does the holding compare in size and statistical power with the published studies?
- Summary: The 212-source manifest is cross-referenced against 114 prior research reports to say what is held by task: the large vertebra-labelled CT releases, about 20,280 transitional-screening CTs, 9,108 level-labelled real plain films, and exactly one real intraoperative human set with a volume (C-ArmLSpine). What is missing most is labelled real intraoperative frames from living patients (LevelCheck cohort, Vanderbilt, Huashan, PUMCH, SX-Stitch), all request-only with no drafted request recorded as sent, and seven gated CT collections, Merlin alone expected to add more transitional people than tier 1. Size ratios against comparable studies are larger for radiograph level identification and C-arm naming, smaller for registration, human readers, 2-D LSTV and prevalence. Every powered statement so far rests on planning accuracies of 95 to 99% that no transitional measurement supports; measured transitional figures (render L6 recall 56.1 to 72.4%, 49.9% on anomalous film windows) put every transitional test near zero power, and device-level claims cannot be tested with 4 devices. Expected transitional counts under the acquisition plan's priors are set beside calls so far, and the page lists the unknowns that change a decision and the reading it uses where two files disagree.
- Key claims:
  - (measured) The holding has exactly one real intraoperative human set with a volume.
  - (measured) Labelled real intraoperative frames from living patients are the missing set that matters most, and no drafted request is recorded as sent.
  - (inferred) Merlin alone is expected to add more transitional people than all of tier 1.
  - (inferred) The holding is larger than the comparable radiograph level-identification studies but accuracy cannot be ranked.
  - (inferred) Every powered statement rests on planning accuracies no transitional measurement supports.
  - (inferred) Device-level claims cannot be tested with 4 devices.
  - (measured) The one comparison already decided at its n is held-out-device against same-device detection.
  - (measured) The COBRA2026 one-labeller screen is not trusted.
  - (inferred) T13 is the scarce class everywhere; L6 is not short in number but in verification.
  - (measured) The frozen paper-2 endpoint held on 57.0% of ordinary development frames and the fixed sacrum detector found the sacrum on 3.4% of COBRA2026 frames before the detectors were replaced.
- Numbers (64 recorded; the first 10 here):
  - 212 sources: The data manifest cross-referenced

  - 1,005; 374; 1,228 and 1,935; 660; 2,019; 802 CTs / scans: Vertebra-labelled CT held: CTSpine1K, VerSe, TotalSegmentator v2 and v3, RibFrac, RSNA 2022, CTSpinoPelvic1K
  - about 20,280 of 21,622 CTs: Transitional-anatomy CT on disk; about 11,770 people countable at the lumbosacral junction; nobody has read them
  - 149 / 299 / 602 cases / spot frames / sweep projections: C-ArmLSpine, the one human intraoperative set; DeepFluoro 366, Geneva 116, Ljubljana 18
  - 692 / 375,618 cases / projections: COBRA2026; 145 cases in any frame bank; Varian not exported

  - 1,260 vs 831 expected transitional people: Merlin at the low prior against tier 1

### 18. Research directions from the data holding

- Folder: `SchwingNet/artifacts/research-directions-from-the-data-holding-kcyckb`; account: second account; link id: KCyCkBtrHXGYQVM6LtpnK8; docs, roadmap; date: 2026-10-08 (Header line under the title: "Oct 8, 2026 . @[the owner]"; the text also cites "the 2026-10-08 rerun" of the 3-D feat...); confidentiality: mixed.
- Question: Given the data the spine programme holds today, which research projects should run in what order, with what gate, n, outside check and dependency, and which unknowns still decide each one?
- Summary: A research-directions page dated Oct 8, 2026, condensed from six reader notes and three independent proposals and checked by three reviewers against 711 claims, that orders the programme's work: measurement first, human reads second, models third. It finds the 2-D naming system at ceiling on ordinary spines (97.4 % of bodies on a whole lateral frame, 74.8 % with one body in view) but with zero verified real-frame transitional test persons, and argues that the published 3-D 0.990 T12-vs-L1 number is a within-patient, coronal-feature result that cannot yet serve as the parity target. It tables every mechanism tried on transitional spines with its measured result, the fluoroscopy, paired, CT and unused data sources with their restrictions, the pre-stated gates against today's n, and eleven CT projects with their gates and outside checks. It lays out a three-stage visibility experiment (single-vertebra 3-D target on the laptop, projected morphometry at exact pose on the 802 v11 volumes, then real frames) with ICC and AUC gates, five model variants held until stage 2 reports, and a ranked list of sixteen projects with a week-one order in which no model trains. It closes with where the three proposals disagreed and a table of the unknowns that decide each project and where each was looked for.
- Key claims:
  - (inferred) The evidence supports one order of work: measurement first, human reads second, models third.
  - (measured) On ordinary spines the 2-D system is at ceiling on plain film and near ceiling on the one real C-arm.
  - (measured) No number exists on a real transitional frame with a human read; the verified real-frame transitional test count is zero.
  - (inferred) The published 3-D 0.990 for T12 against L1 is not a single-vertebra classifier, and its dominant feature is coronal and invisible on a lateral.
  - (inferred) No lumbosacral 3-D classifier exists at all.
  - (measured) Every morphology-only mechanism measured below ordinary-spine accuracy; every counting mechanism was measured only on ordinary spines or renders; the two were never coupled on a real transitional frame.
  - (measured) Render-to-fluoro translation (SDXL, CUT, CycleGAN, ADDA), Star Temporal Classification and size in millimetres as a scalar are refuted by the record.
  - (measured) The dominant failure on rendered L6 spines is a whole-run shift one level cranial, exactly the error a count catches.
  - (measured) The current encoder is leaked: 92 of 97 transitional validation and test persons sit in the v8 and v9 training splits, so a clean encoder must restart from ImageNet on G3 training persons only.
  - (measured) Mixed training is the default because pretrain-then-fine-tune lost 25.6 anomalous points, though whether that refutation was re-run on a clean bank is unknown.
  - (inferred) The 85 % variant-sensitivity gate (22 carriers with no miss) is practically unreachable at the measured recall.
  - (inferred) The first clean model is expected to fail its validation screen, and that failure is itself a result to state plainly.
- Numbers (81 recorded; the first 10 here):
  - 97.4 % of bodies: 2-D naming of bodies on a whole lateral frame, ordinary spines, the one real C-arm
  - 74.8 % of bodies: 2-D naming with one body in view on the real C-arm
  - 0 persons: verified real-frame transitional test count today
  - 56.1 to 72.4 % L6 recall, n = 10 held-out persons: best rendered L6 recall
  - 32 vs 76 % sensitivity: human readers for Castellvi type on radiographs vs CT (Hanhivaara 2024)
  - 0.990 AUC: published 3-D T12 against L1 discrimination; five of eleven features divided by the case median
  - 802 labelled CTs: exact-pose renders for the projection experiment (CTSpinoPelvic1K v11)
  - 711 claims: claims opened and verified by three reviewers for the full source document
  - 19 mm: Castellvi type I transverse-process height threshold; type I is 68 % of all LSTV and unrecoverable from a lateral
  - 9 to 11 % of CTs: count-changing Castellvi types II to IV, the target
- Datasets: C-ArmLSpine (Surgio-B51), COBRA2026 (Elekta XVI, Varian), DeepFluoro, BUU-LSPINE, CSXA, NHANES II, VinDr-SpineXR, ctsp_drr, physics_drr, physics_rare, ts_drr, ts_drr_v3, verse_drr, CTSpinoPelvic1K v11 (and the v10 public mirror), CTSpine1K, COLONOG, VerSe and VerSeFusion (Loeffler table), TotalSegmentator v2 and v3, the acquisition tiers (tier 1), Dataset803, Dataset813, Dataset815, RibFrac, RibSeg v2, Moller stump masks, RSNA 2022 cervical, AASCE, Spinal-AI, anchor_drr, Mendeley (a BUU redistribution), lateral_236, Ljubljana cadaveric L1 to L5, Geneva, CTPelvic1K, LevelCheck clinical cohort, Huashan, SX-Stitch, Balgrist ex-vivo, the fourteen cine sets, Charters 2024 (UCLA), Jaganathan and Maier CBCT sweeps, TCIA CMB-MML and CMB-AML XA, NIT Warangal, Merlin (Stanford AIMI), CT-RATE, INSPECT, NLST, FluoroCap, the G3 person-disjoint split; tools: the separability script (ratio-only variant), the ALPHABET_LOSS protocol, the coupling script, the benchmark collector, the pipeline-state tool on the grid, labels_for, the ostk toolkit (commit 1b7005c), SPINEPS subregion labels, nnU-Net raw datasets, RTK geometry, the COBRA exporter, the two-stage review tree, the batch STATUS file on the Ceph volume, Slurm (reqp partition), singularity container, the Hugging Face mirror (v10), Weights from ImageNet, Wilson lower bound, conformal sets, Star Temporal Classification, SDXL, CUT, CycleGAN, ADDA restyling, the leak audit, the eval gate; models: the 2-D naming classifier (v8, v9, v11 lineages), the clean-lineage ResNet-18 two-channel encoder (FLU-2), the rule counting decoder, the anchored decode with T13 and L6 columns, the alphabet-loss head (lambda 0 and 1.0), the binary LSTV and TLTV whole-frame flag, the vertebra detector, the 3-D shape model (T12 vs L1, ratio-only), the anchor-ordinal design (CT-7), the super-detector flag (CT-9), the LS-L6 segmentation classifier (CT-4), Kwak 2025 2-D LSTV model, Schinz 2026, VERIDAH, HealthOST, Moller's stump-rib detector, Payer 2020's landmark network, SyntheX, TotalSegmentator.
- Decisions:
  - The standing determination: name the level correctly on a transitional spine (L6, T13, sacralised L5, four lumbar) from C-arm exposures at parity with an ordinary spine, and report "not at parity" plainly if that is the result. (2026-09-23): Narrower and stronger than "anomaly-aware"; Castellvi type I is unrecoverable from a lateral by construction, so the target is the count-changing types II to...
  - The page takes proposal B as the programme, grafting A's smallest-experiment-that-changes-a-decision rule and its ordering of the ratio-only run first, and C's venue and power discipline. (2026-10-08), the author (the owner): The three asks are B's structure and its feature-level specification is what a student can act on.
  - The 85 % bound on the 43 test L6 persons allows one miss, not two. (2026-10-08), the author (the owner): 42 of 43 gives 87.9 %, 41 of 43 gives 84.5 %.
  - Mixed training (renders and real films in every batch) is the default schedule.: Pretrain-then-fine-tune lost 25.6 anomalous points.
  - The clean encoder starts from ImageNet on G3 training persons only, with no decoder pseudolabels and FluoroCap in no training list.: 92 of 97 transitional validation and test persons sit in the v8 and v9 training splits.
  - Excluded by the record: Star Temporal Classification, CycleGAN, ADDA, CUT and SDXL restyling, decoder-named pseudolabels, size in millimetres as a scalar, and a learned refiner as the 2-D reference.: Each returned no gain or was refuted in measured arms.
  - Stop rule for FLU-2: zero or one miss of the 7 render-scoreable validation L6 persons proceeds to three seeds and two heads; two or more misses stops the fluoroscopy arm and the manuscript says "not at parity" with the number.: At a true recall of 72.4 % the screen passes 38 % of the time, at 85 % 72 %.
  - No G3 test person is scored until paper 2's ledger carries an "H1 seen" entry; if the read confirms fewer than 22 carriers with an exported frame, no fluoroscopy transitional sensitivity is claimed and the render number is reported alone.: The data requests are the only route to 22.
  - No weight trained with BUU laterals leaves until the owner has read the licence., the owner: The BUU EULA forbids redistribution and the licence reading for derived labels is open.
  - Each model variant V0 to V4 is built only after stage 2 has said which features a view carries and after the owner's decision., the owner: None starts until the visibility experiment has reported.
